Relational parsing: a clean, fast parsing strategy for all context-free languages Thanks: This manuscript is a work in progress: a few proofs still need to be written down, and there are many rough edges. We also hope to provide more experimental results and extend the discussion in the final section. Thus, please contact us first if you would like to cite this work. We also welcome comments and suggestions of improvements, regarding both the algorithm itself and the way it is presented here.
Grzegorz Herman

Abstract
We present a novel parsing algorithm for all context-free languages, based on computing the relation between configurations and reaching transitions in a recursive transition network. Parsing complexity w.r.t. input length matches the state of the art: it is worst-case cubic, quadratic for unambiguous grammars, and linear for LR-regular ones. What distinguishes our algorithm is its clean mathematical formulation: parsing is expressed as a composition of simple operations on languages and relations, and can therefore be implemented using only immutable data structures. With a proper choice of these structures, a vast majority of operations performed during parsing typical programming languages can be memoized, which allows our proof-of-concept implementation to outperform common generalized parsing algorithms, in some cases by orders of magnitude.



1
Introduction

Parsing is a well-studied and yet still active topic in computer science.
Multiple highly efficient deterministic parsers can handle large families of unambiguous context-free grammars, and oftentimes more.
However, grammars of many programming languages do not naturally fit within the limitations required by these parsers, and need tedious manual adaptation
(e.g., replacing left recursion with right recursion).
Worse, such adaptation needs to be
“undone”
on parse trees, in order to make them reflect the desired structure of the language.
Further, in many cases the
“natural”
grammar of a language allows some ambiguity, which is easier resolved at a later compilation step.
With the growing need for rapid development of new languages,
generalized parsers—able to handle all, even ambiguous, context-free grammars—are gaining wider adoption.

Most generalized parsing techniques are based on clever parallel simulation of nondeterministic choices of a pushdown automaton.
Some of these algorithms offer good complexity guarantees: worst-case cubic, quadratic on unambiguous grammars, and in some cases linear on
“nice”
language fragments.
Nondeterminism is handled by means of (some variant of) a data structure called GSS (graph-structured stack).
This makes the constant factors involved significantly larger than in deterministic parsers, which use a simple stack.
Moreover, in order to handle the full class of context-free grammars,
the graph-like data structures in
all
these algorithms need to be
mutable
and allow
cycles
.
Even where it does not significantly complicate the implementation,
it makes reasoning about the algorithm more difficult.

We present a new generalized context-free parsing algorithm which we call
relational parsing
.
Similarly to existing approaches, it simulates possible runs of a pushdown automaton (specifically, a recursive transition network).
However, its formulation is based on precise mathematical semantics, given in Section
2
:
it inductively computes the languages of configurations reachable after reading consecutive input symbols.
We show that each such language can be obtained from the previous one by a constant number of simple, well-known language-theoretic operations.
The way of representing these languages (and their grammar-dependent atomic building blocks) is left abstract at this point.

Our recognition algorithm generalizes cleanly to a full-fledged parser, capable of producing a compact representation of all possible parse trees.
To achieve that, instead of languages of reachable configurations, we compute the relations between configurations and the ways in which they can be reached
(hence the algorithm name).
This generalization is presented in Section
3
.
Again, the representation of relations is hidden behind an abstract API.

We give possible concrete realizations of all required abstract data types in Section
4
.
For the relations computed during parsing, we use a DAG structure akin to the graph-structured stack.
However, with our approach, all cyclicity is embedded in the atomic relations,
and thus we
never
need to add edges to existing DAG nodes.
The atomic languages and relations are shown to be regular,
and thus can be represented by nondeterministic finite automata.
We also discuss some possible representations of transition languages.

Instead of computing the language of all accepting transition sequences,
our parsing algorithm can work with any semiring induced by individual transitions.
However, as presented, it introduces artificial ambiguity into the objects computed,
and thus requires such semiring to be idempotent.
In Section
5
we pinpoint the reasons for this ambiguity,
and show how it can be eliminated by a simple yet subtle adjustment of the atomic relations.

The input-related complexity of our algorithm, discussed in Section
6
, matches the state of the art:
our parser is worst-case cubic, quadratic on unambiguous grammars, and linear on LR-regular grammars.

There are multiple benefits of our clean, semantic approach.
The automata representing atomic languages can be optimized.
More importantly, due to immutability, partial results can be memoized and reused.
In Section
7
we outline how to achieve such reuse by using a more complex representation of languages.
On real-world inputs (Java 8 grammar and a large collection of source files)
it allows a
vast majority
of phases to be performed on a plain stack,
which makes our (unoptimized) proof-of-concept C
♯
implementation significantly outperform (our, unoptimized implementation of) GLL.
In recognition mode the speed-up is close to
two orders of magnitude
—the running times are on par with those of the hand-coded deterministic parser used by the Java compiler.
This experimental evaluation is presented in Section
8
.

Finally, in Section
9
we discuss how our approach fits into the generalized parsing landscape.
We show how the bits and pieces of our algorithm relate to the seminal work of Lang, Earley, and Tomita,
as well as to GLL, GLR, GLC (generalized left-corner), reduction-incorporated parsers, and Adaptive LL(*) employed by ANTLR4.
Within the discussion we also hint at some places where our algorithm could be improved, or our approach extended.

Our proof-of-concept implementation can be accessed at

https://gitlab.com/alt-lang/research/relational-parsing
.

2
Recognition

There are many equivalent formalisms capturing context-free languages.
In this work, we use
recursive transition networks

[
25
]
,
a restricted class of nondeterministic pushdown automata.
They directly model the computational behavior of (nondeterministic) recursive descent parsers,
and at the same time can be
trivially
obtained from context-free grammars.
1
1

1

This includes popular variants of context-free grammars in which regular expressions can be used at right-hand sides of production rules.

Importantly, the states obtained in such translation are exactly the
“items”
used by GLL, (G)LC, and (G)LR parsers.
Let us begin with a quick review of the chosen model.

1
.

A
recursive transition network
over a terminal alphabet
A
and set of production labels
2
2

2

The labels are never looked at by our algorithm.
However, they can be attached to internal parse tree nodes—a role usually served by grammar productions, hence the name.

P
consists of:

•

a finite set
S
of states, with a distinguished start state
s_{\textup{start}}\in S
,

•

a finite set
D
of transitions, each being one of:

–

\textup{{shift}}\langle s,a,t\rangle
with
s,t\in S
and
a\in A
,

–

\textup{{call}}\langle s,u,t\rangle
with
s,u,t\in S
, or

–

\textup{{reduce}}\langle s,p\rangle
with
s\in S
and
p\in P
.

Throughout the paper, we assume to be working with a single network,
and thus consider
A
,
S
,
D
and
P
to be fixed.
For convenience, we let
D_{a}
denote the set of shifts for each particular symbol
a\in A
,
and
D_{\epsilon}
—the set of all non-shift transitions.

Recognition by an RTN begins in the state
s_{\textup{start}}
.
At each moment, the network may nondeterministically choose any legal transition from its current state
s
.
A
\textup{{shift}}\langle s,a,t\rangle
consumes the symbol
a
from the input and changes state to
t
,

\textup{{call}}\langle s,u,t\rangle
recursively invokes recognition from state
u
and moves to
t
once the recursive call returns,
and
\textup{{reduce}}\langle s,p\rangle
returns from a single level of recursion.
An input
\alpha
belongs to the language of the RTN
if there is a sequence of legal transitions which consumes the whole
\alpha
,
and ends by returning from the top recursion level.
If a parse tree is to be constructed,
its shape follows exactly the nesting of recursive calls made,
with
p
from
\textup{{reduce}}\langle s,p\rangle
used to label the resulting node.

Making explicit the recursion stack, the recognition process can be formalized as follows:

2
.

A
configuration
of a recursive transition network is a stack of its states, i.e. a member of
S^{*}

(the top—current—state is the first in such sequence).
Each transition
d\in D
induces the following
reachability
relation

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\sigma.

Reachability generalizes to sequences of transitions (by composing relations for consecutive transitions),
and then to languages of transitions (by taking union of relations for individual transition sequences).
A sequence of transitions is
viable
if it induces a nonempty reachability relation.

Our recognition algorithm will simulate the nondeterministic choices made by the RTN
by computing the sets of configurations reachable after consuming consecutive symbols from the input.
To avoid dealing with empty configurations, we augment the set of states
S
by a fresh state
s_{\textup{stop}}
(with no outgoing transitions)
and use it as a guard marking the bottom of each configuration.

3
.

For an input
\alpha\in A^{*}
,
the
parsing language

\Sigma_{\alpha}\subseteq S^{*}

consists of configurations (augmented with
s_{\textup{stop}}
) reachable from the initial configuration when parsing
\alpha
:

\Sigma_{a_{1}{\cdots}a_{n}}\triangleq\{\sigma{\cdot}s_{\textup{stop}}:\ s_{\textup{start}}\mathrel{\hbox to81.34pt{\vbox to18.27pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 40.67113pt\lower-12.19118pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

The question of whether an input belongs to the language recognized by the network
is now the same as whether its parsing language contains the configuration
s_{\textup{stop}}
.
The following definition allows constructing this language inductively, symbol by symbol:

4
.

The
call-reduce closure
and
shift by a symbol
a\in A
of a language
\Sigma\subseteq S^{*}

are the sets of configurations reachable from
\Sigma
without shifts and by shifting
a
, respectively:

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)

\Sigma_{\epsilon}=\mathcal{C}(s_{\textup{start}}){\cdot}s_{\textup{stop}}=\mathcal{C}(s_{\textup{start}}){\cdot}\mathcal{C}(s_{\textup{stop}})

\Sigma_{\textup{out}}

Let us begin with a naïve implementation, presented as Algorithm
2
.
While obviously correct, it is not directly usable,
as the loop in line 2 ranges over potentially infinite
\Sigma
.
To deal with this issue, we rephrase it in terms of language derivatives.

5
.

A
derivative of degree
k
of a language
\Sigma\subseteq S^{*}
by a symbol
s\in S

is any collection of languages
\Sigma^{\textup{}(s)}=\{\Sigma_{i}\}_{i=1\ldots k}

such that for each
\sigma\in S^{*}
we have
3
3

3

On the right-hand side of the equivalence,
s
is completely redundant.
We keep it for consistency with later generalizations of this definition.

s{\cdot}\sigma\in\Sigma\iff s{\cdot}\sigma\in\bigcup_{i}s{\cdot}\Sigma_{i}.

A language is
(m,k)
-regular
if it belongs to some family of size
m
, closed under derivatives of degree
k
.
It is
k
-regular
if it is
(m,k)
-regular for some (finite)
m
, and
regular
if it is
k
-regular for some
k
.

This definition of regular languages is equivalent to the standard one.
The parameter
m
measures the size of an automaton recognizing such language,
while
k
—the degree of its nondeterminism.
In particular, derivatives of degree one are exactly those of Brzozowski
[
3
]
.

Derivatives let us look at the first (top) state in a configuration,
keeping everything that lies below it encapsulated.
This leads to Algorithm
3
which, while still operating on potentially infinite objects,
no longer needs to explicitly examine their contents.
As we will show in Section
4
, all languages encountered during recognition are regular,
and thus have a finite representation on which the necessary operations can be performed efficiently.

3

\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in S^{*}
we have

\sigma_{1}{\cdot}s{\cdot}\sigma_{2}\in\Sigma\implies\sigma_{1}{\cdot}\sigma_{2}\in\Sigma.

The
null closure
of a language
\Sigma
is the smallest null-closed language
\overline{\Sigma}\supseteq\Sigma
.

Now, for null-closed
\Sigma
, the phase algorithm can be rewritten as Algorithm
4
.
Lines 5–7 are necessary because the state
s^{\prime}
could have entered the language
“in the middle”
of a call-reduce closure,
and thus might itself not have been call-reduce closed.

1
and replace all call-reduce closures by their null closures.
The correctness of such change needs to be justified—we do so by the following reasoning.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on the length of
\eta
.
If
\eta=\epsilon
, the decomposition is
\eta_{\scriptscriptstyle-}=\epsilon
(with
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle-}=\epsilon
) and
\eta_{\scriptscriptstyle+}=\epsilon
(with
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle+}=s_{\scriptscriptstyle+}
being any state in
S
).

Otherwise, let
s_{0}
be the source state of the first transition in
\eta
.
If
s_{0}\mathrel{\hbox to9.23pt{\vbox to14.8pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.61285pt\lower-10.45659pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

∎

2
.

Then there must exist
\sigma_{1}{\cdot}s{\cdot}\sigma_{2}\in\Sigma

with
\epsilon\in\overline{\sigma_{1}}
and
\sigma\in\overline{\sigma_{2}}
.
As
\Sigma
is call-reduce-closed, we have

,
from which

and
t{\cdot}\sigma\in\overline{{\mathcal{S}_{a}(\Sigma)}}
as desired.
∎

3
.

for every language

.

from which
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle+}{\cdot}\sigma_{2}\in\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)

and
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle+}{\cdot}\sigma^{\prime\prime}\in\overline{\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)}
as desired.
∎

4
.

\overline{\Sigma_{\epsilon}}=\overline{\mathcal{C}(s_{\textup{start}}{\cdot}s_{\textup{stop}})}
and
\overline{\Sigma_{\alpha a}}=\overline{\mathcal{C}({\mathcal{S}_{a}(\overline{\Sigma_{\alpha}})})}
.

Proof.

The first equality follows directly from the definition of
\Sigma_{\epsilon}
.
The second can be obtained by combining Lemmas
2
and
3
,
idempotency of null closure, and monotonicity of null closure, shift and call-reduce closure into the following:

\overline{\mathcal{C}({\mathcal{S}_{a}(\overline{\Sigma_{\alpha}})})}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{C}(\overline{{\mathcal{S}_{a}(\Sigma_{\alpha})}})}\subseteq\overline{\overline{\mathcal{C}({\mathcal{S}_{a}(\Sigma_{\alpha})})}}=\overline{\overline{\Sigma_{\alpha a}}}=\overline{\Sigma_{\alpha a}}=\overline{\mathcal{C}({\mathcal{S}_{a}(\Sigma_{\alpha})})}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{C}({\mathcal{S}_{a}(\overline{\Sigma_{\alpha}})})}.

∎

As a consequence, plugging Algorithm
4
into Algorithm
1
(with all call-reduce closures replaced by their null closures)
we obtain a procedure for context-free recognition,
realizable using any data type
\mathbb{\Sigma}
representing languages over
S
, implementing API
5
.

API 5
.

Operations required for recognition.

•

\texttt{root}()\in\mathbb{\Sigma}

\{\epsilon\}
,

•

\texttt{prepend}(\overline{\mathcal{C}(s)};\Sigma\in\mathbb{\Sigma})\in\mathbb{\Sigma}

\overline{\mathcal{C}(s)}{\cdot}\Sigma
,

•

\texttt{union}(\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2}\in\mathbb{\Sigma})\in\mathbb{\Sigma}

\Sigma_{1}\cup\Sigma_{2}
,

•

\texttt{epsilon}(\Sigma\in\mathbb{\Sigma})\in\{\texttt{true},\texttt{false}\}

whether
\epsilon\in\Sigma
,

•

\texttt{derivative}(\Sigma\in\mathbb{\Sigma};s\in S)\in\mathbb{\Sigma}^{\leqslant k}

\Sigma^{\textup{}(s)}
.

3
Parsing

We now augment our algorithm to perform generalized parsing and not just mere recognition.
The exposition here mirrors closely the one from the previous section.
All definitions and algorithms are adjusted so that instead of asking
whether
some configuration is reachable,
we ask about
the language of transitions
by which it can be reached.
In particular, instead of computing (a representation of) all possible parse trees,
we are going to output the language of all legal transition sequences.

7
.

For an input
\alpha\in A^{*}
,
the
parsing relation

\Phi_{\alpha}\subseteq S^{*}{\times}D^{*}

associates configurations reachable when parsing
\alpha

with reversed
4
4

4

Tracking how an RTN configuration evolves under a sequence of transitions,
one can see that the states at the
beginning
(top) of the ultimate configuration depend primarily on the transitions at the
end
of the sequence.
Thus, to keep the direction of concatenation consistent, we record the transition history in reverse.

sequences of transitions by which they can be reached from the initial configuration:

\alpha

are exactly (reversed) those which
\Phi_{\alpha}
associates with the configuration
s_{\textup{stop}}

Parsing relations for a particular input can be calculated inductively analogously to parsing languages,
because
\Phi_{\epsilon}=\mathcal{C}(s_{\textup{start}}{\cdot}s_{\textup{stop}})
and
\Phi_{\alpha a}=\mathcal{C}({\mathcal{S}_{a}(\Phi_{\alpha})})
.
To efficiently perform a single phase according to the latter equality, we need a suitable notion of derivatives:

9
.

A
derivative of degree
k
of a relation
\Phi\subseteq S^{*}{\times}D^{*}
by a symbol
s\in S

is any collection of pairs
\Phi^{\textup{}(s)}=\{(\Delta_{i},\Phi_{i})\}_{i=1\ldots k}

with
\Delta_{i}\subseteq D^{*}
and
\Phi_{i}\subseteq S^{*}{\times}D^{*}
,
such that for each
\sigma\in S^{*}
and
\delta\in D^{*}
we have

(s{\cdot}\sigma,{\hbox to4.84pt{\vbox to10.82pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-3.47221pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

A relation is
(m,k)
-regular
if it belongs to some family of size
m
, closed under derivatives of degree
k
.
It is
k
-regular
if it is
(m,k)
-regular for some
m
, and
regular
if it is
k
-regular for some
k
.

Regular relations, as defined here, are similar to
rational relations
—a well-studied generalization of regular languages
[
5
]
.
The fundamental difference is that the latter require the components to be
“jointly regular”
,
whereas we put no restriction on the transition languages
\Delta_{i}
.
We are only concerned about finiteness (and bounded nondeterminism) of the automaton with respect to
S
.
Note however, that despite this flexibility, such automata are not always determinizable.

6
.

Calculating
\mathcal{C}({\mathcal{S}_{a}(\Phi)})

⬇

1

\Phi_{\textup{out}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\varnothing

2

foreach

d=\textup{{shift}}\langle s,a,t\rangle\in D_{a}
:

3

foreach

(\Delta_{0},\Phi_{0})\in\Phi^{\textup{}(s)}
:

4

\Phi_{\textup{out}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Phi_{\textup{out}}\ \cup\ \mathcal{C}(t){\cdot}d{\cdot}\Delta_{0}{\cdot}\Phi_{0}

5

if

\Delta_{t\rightsquigarrow\epsilon}\neq\varnothing
,

foreach

s_{1}\in S
:

6

foreach

(\Delta_{1},\Phi_{1})\in\Phi_{0}^{\textup{}(s_{1})}
:

7

\Phi_{\textup{out}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Phi_{\textup{out}}\ \cup\ \mathcal{C}(s_{1}){\cdot}\Delta_{t\rightsquigarrow\epsilon}{\cdot}d{\cdot}\Delta_{0}{\cdot}\Delta_{1}{\cdot}\Phi_{1}

8

if

\Delta_{s_{1}\rightsquigarrow\epsilon}\neq\varnothing
,

foreach

s_{2}\in S
:

9

foreach

(\Delta_{2},\Phi_{2})\in\Phi_{1}^{\textup{}(s_{2})}
:

10

\Phi_{\textup{out}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Phi_{\textup{out}}\ \cup\ \mathcal{C}(s_{2}){\cdot}\Delta_{s_{1}\rightsquigarrow\epsilon}{\cdot}\Delta_{t\rightsquigarrow\epsilon}{\cdot}d{\cdot}\Delta_{0}{\cdot}\Delta_{1}{\cdot}\Delta_{2}{\cdot}\Phi_{2}

11

\ldots

12

return

\Phi_{\textup{out}}

Algorithm
6
(completely analogous to Algorithm
3
) can now be used to calculate
\mathcal{C}({\mathcal{S}_{a}(\Phi)})
.
Adapting it to have constant depth is however not as easy as before,
because skipping nullable states requires additional transitions to be performed.
To handle this, we enrich the alphabet of configurations to

G\triangleq S\cup D_{\epsilon},

allowing them to intersperse states with (non-shift) transitions.
Such transitions will
“guide”
the simulation of the RTN—if present on top of a configuration, they will need to be executed (en bloc) before the states below them can be accessed.
To express that, reachability is enriched by

\eta{\cdot}\gamma\mathrel{\hbox to32.56pt{\vbox to18.13pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 16.28139pt\lower-12.12523pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

Note, that guiding transitions will be introduced into configurations only artificially, replacing states which we commit to null later on—reachability may only remove them, fulfilling that commitment.

We now consider
guided languages
(languages of guided configurations, i.e., subsets of
G^{*}
)
and
guided relations
(subsets of
G^{*}{\times}D^{*}
),
and (re)define null closure to optionally replace states by their nulling transitions:

The
null closure
of a language
\Gamma
(relation
\Psi
) is the smallest null-closed guided language
\overline{\Gamma}\supseteq\Gamma
(guided relation
\overline{\Psi}\supseteq\Psi
).

We now revisit and augment the reasoning from the previous section
to show that an inductive procedure can be used to compute the null closures of parsing relations.
First, we make a convenient observation about the interplay between performing transitions and null closure:

5
.

\gamma\in G^{*}

\gamma^{\prime}\in\overline{\gamma}
.

with
\gamma^{\prime}_{2}\in\overline{\gamma_{2}}
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on the length of
\delta
.
The case when
\delta=\epsilon
is trivial.
Otherwise,
\gamma^{\prime}
and
\gamma
must be nonempty.
We distinguish three cases.

If
\gamma=d{\cdot}\gamma_{1}
for some
d\in D_{\epsilon}
and
\gamma_{1}\in G^{*}
, we have

6
.

{\mathcal{S}_{a}(\overline{\Psi})}\subseteq\overline{{\mathcal{S}_{a}(\Psi)}}
for every guided relation
\Psi\subseteq G^{*}{\times}D^{*}
.

7
.

\mathcal{C}(\overline{\Psi})\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{C}(\Psi)}
for every guided relation
\Psi\subseteq G^{*}{\times}D^{*}
.

8
.

Null closure preserves being call-reduce-closed:

\mathcal{C}(\Psi)=\Psi\implies\mathcal{C}(\overline{\Psi})=\overline{\Psi}.

Proof.

\mathcal{C}(\overline{\Psi})=\mathcal{C}(\overline{\mathcal{C}(\Psi)})\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{C}(\Psi))}=\overline{\mathcal{C}(\Psi)}=\overline{\Psi}\subseteq\mathcal{C}(\overline{\Psi})
.
∎

9
.

\overline{\Phi_{\epsilon}}=\overline{\mathcal{C}(s_{\textup{start}}{\cdot}s_{\textup{stop}})}
and
\overline{\Phi_{\alpha a}}=\overline{\mathcal{C}({\mathcal{S}_{a}(\overline{\Phi_{\alpha}})})}
.

Proof.

Redo the proof of Proposition
4
,
using Corollaries
6
and
7
instead of Lemmas
2
and
3
.
∎

To proceed to phase computation, we define two variants of derivatives for guided relations:
the first one, analogous to Definition
9
, separates the guiding transitions,
while the second, weaker, concatenates them to the remaining relation:

A guided relation is
(m,k)
-regular
if it belongs to some family of size
m
, closed under derivatives of degree
k
.
It is
k
-regular
if it is
(m,k)
-regular for some
m
, and
regular
if it is
k
-regular for some
k
.

.

Calculating
\overline{\mathcal{C}({\mathcal{S}_{a}(\Psi)})}
for call-reduce-closed, proper
\Psi

⬇

1

\Psi_{\textup{out}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\varnothing

2

foreach

d=\textup{{shift}}\langle s,a,t\rangle\in D_{a}
:

3

foreach

\Psi_{0}\in\Psi^{\textup{flat}(s)}
:

4

\Psi_{\textup{out}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Psi_{\textup{out}}\ \cup\ \overline{\mathcal{C}(t)}{\cdot}d{\cdot}\Psi_{0}

5

if

\Delta_{t\rightsquigarrow\epsilon}\neq\varnothing
,

foreach

s^{\prime}\in S
:

6

foreach

\Psi_{1}\in(\Delta_{t\rightsquigarrow\epsilon}{\cdot}d{\cdot}\Psi_{0})^{\textup{flat}(s^{\prime})}
:

7

\Psi_{\textup{out}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Psi_{\textup{out}}\ \cup\ \overline{\mathcal{C}(s^{\prime})}{\cdot}\Psi_{1}

8

return

\Psi_{\textup{out}}

When transitions are forgotten, both above definitions degenerate to Brzozowski derivatives.
The distinction is necessary for clean separation of concerns in our algorithms,
and for their complexity analysis.
In particular, only the weaker notion of derivatives is needed to calculate null closures of parsing relations,
as shown in Algorithm
7
.
We only prove its correctness for guided relations which are
proper
,
i.e., can be obtained as null closures of non-guided relations.

10
.

For a call-reduce-closed, proper
\Psi=\overline{\Phi}

in which every configuration has the state
s_{\textup{stop}}
at its bottom,
Algorithm
7
correctly returns
\Psi_{\textup{out}}=\overline{\mathcal{C}({\mathcal{S}_{a}(\Psi)})}
.

Proof.

Claim 1:
\mathcal{C}({\mathcal{S}_{a}(\Phi)})\subseteq\Psi_{\textup{out}}
.

\mathcal{C}({\mathcal{S}_{a}(\Phi)})
must come from

\displaystyle\ \in\overline{\mathcal{C}({\mathcal{S}_{a}(\Psi)})}.

Finale:

Combine the above claims with Corollaries
6
and
7
to obtain

\overline{\mathcal{C}({\mathcal{S}_{a}(\Psi)})}=\overline{\mathcal{C}({\mathcal{S}_{a}(\overline{\Phi})})}\subseteq\overline{\overline{\mathcal{C}({\mathcal{S}_{a}(\Phi)})}}\subseteq\overline{\overline{\Psi}_{\textup{out}}}=\Psi_{\textup{out}}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{C}({\mathcal{S}_{a}(\Psi)})}.

∎

8
.

Calculating
\overline{\mathcal{C}({\mathcal{S}_{a}(\Psi)})}
for call-reduce-closed, proper
\Psi
(final)

⬇

1

\Psi_{\textup{out}},\Psi_{\textup{aux}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\varnothing

foreach

:

3

foreach

:

4

\Psi_{\textup{out}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Psi_{\textup{out}}\ \cup\ \overline{\mathcal{C}(t)}{\cdot}d{\cdot}\Psi_{0}

5

if

\Delta_{t\rightsquigarrow\epsilon}\neq\varnothing
:

6

\Psi_{\textup{aux}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Psi_{\textup{aux}}\ \cup\ \Delta_{t\rightsquigarrow\epsilon}{\cdot}d{\cdot}\Psi_{0}

7

foreach

s^{\prime}\in S
:

8

foreach

\Psi_{1}\in\Psi_{\textup{aux}}^{\textup{flat}(s^{\prime})}
:

9

\Psi_{\textup{out}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Psi_{\textup{out}}\ \cup\ \overline{\mathcal{C}(s^{\prime})}{\cdot}\Psi_{1}

10

return

\Psi_{\textup{out}}

One last observation we apply is that derivatives distribute over union,
and thus the inner loops in lines 5 and 6 can be pulled out to the top level,
resulting in Algorithm
8
.

Extracting the language of accepting transition sequences from the parsing relation for the whole input is also a bit more complicated,
because any remaining guiding transitions need to be executed.
Thus we replace our main Algorithm
1
with Algorithm
9
.
Overall, we realize generalized context-free parsing,
using any representation
\mathbb{\Psi}
of guided relations supporting API
10
.

API 10
.

Operations on relations required for full parsing.

•

\texttt{root}()\in\mathbb{\Psi}

\epsilon
,

•

\texttt{prepend}(\overline{\mathcal{C}(s)};\Psi\in\mathbb{\Psi})\in\mathbb{\Psi}

\overline{\mathcal{C}(s)}{\cdot}\Psi
,

•

\texttt{prepend}(\Delta\subseteq D^{*};\Psi\in\mathbb{\Psi})\in\mathbb{\Psi}

\Delta{\cdot}\Psi
,

•

\texttt{union}(\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2}\in\mathbb{\Psi})\in\mathbb{\Psi}

\Psi_{1}\cup\Psi_{2}
,

•

\texttt{epsilon}(\Psi\in\mathbb{\Psi})\subseteq D^{*}

4
Representations

We now make concrete the key observation underlying this paper:
that although the sets of reachable parser configurations are sometimes infinite,
their regularity makes it possible to manipulate them efficiently.

4.1
Parsing languages and relations

API 11
.

Required operations on atomic languages.

•

\texttt{atomic}(s\in S)\in\mathbb{\Upsilon}

\overline{\mathcal{C}(s)}
,

•

\texttt{epsilon}(\Upsilon\in\mathbb{\Upsilon})\in\{\texttt{true},\texttt{false}\}

whether
\epsilon\in\Upsilon
,

•

\texttt{derivative}(\Upsilon\in\mathbb{\Upsilon},s\in S)\in\mathbb{\Upsilon}^{\leqslant k}

\Upsilon^{\textup{}(s)}
.

We begin top-down, by presenting a data structure implementing API
5
.
To make it independent of the grammar, let us parameterize it with a data type
\mathbb{\Upsilon}
,
representing a finite family of languages over
S
, realizing API
11
.
We allow the first argument of
prepend
to be any element of
\mathbb{\Upsilon}
.

Denote by
\underline{\Sigma}
the
\epsilon
-free part of
\Sigma
(i.e.,
\Sigma-\{\epsilon\}
).
Our implementation is based on the following observation:

11
.

Let
\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2},\ldots,\Sigma_{n}
be a sequence of languages over
S
,
in which each
\Sigma_{i}
is obtained by applying one of the operations of API
5
to some
\Sigma_{j}
with
j<i
.
Then there exists a finite collection of pairs
\{(\Upsilon,i_{j})\}_{j}
with
\Upsilon_{j}\in\mathbb{\Upsilon}
and
1\leqslant i_{j}<n
, such that

\underline{\Sigma_{n}}=\bigcup_{j}\underline{\Upsilon_{j}}{\cdot}\Sigma_{i_{j}}.

Proof.

We argue by induction on
n
.
For languages returned by
derivative
, the inductive step follows by

\underline{\Sigma_{n}^{\textup{}(s)}}\ =\ \underline{\underline{\Sigma_{n}}^{\textup{}(s)}}\ =\ \underline{\bigcup_{j}\bigcup_{\Upsilon^{\prime}\in\underline{\Upsilon_{j}}^{\textup{}(s)}}\Upsilon^{\prime}{\cdot}\Sigma_{i_{j}}}\ =\ \bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\\
\Upsilon^{\prime}\in\Upsilon_{j}^{\textup{}(s)}\end{subarray}}\underline{\Upsilon^{\prime}}{\cdot}\Sigma_{i_{j}}\ \cup\ \bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\\
\epsilon\in\Upsilon^{\prime}\in\Upsilon_{j}^{\textup{}(s)}\end{subarray}}\underline{\Sigma_{i_{j}}},

and applying the inductive hypothesis to the latter part of the sum. All other cases are trivial.
∎

Our proposed data structure keeps, for each
\Sigma_{n}
, the set of pairs warranted above,
and an additional bit denoting whether
\epsilon\in\Sigma_{n}
.
All operations have natural implementations, with
derivative
following the above proof.
Their complexity will be analyzed in Section
6
.
Here, we note only that, as language union is idempotent, any duplicate pairs encountered can and should be merged.
Thus, each
\Sigma_{n}
is represented by at most
n\,|\mathbb{\Upsilon}|
pairs.

The whole structure can be viewed as a DAG, whose vertices correspond to the languages
\Sigma_{i}
.
Edges, labeled with elements of
\mathbb{\Upsilon}
, follow the references from Proposition
11
:
an edge
(u,v)
with label
\Upsilon
is present iff the decomposition of
\underline{\Sigma_{u}}
contains the pair
(\Upsilon,v)
.
Each vertex is additionally labeled with the
\epsilon
bit—vertices at which this bit is set will be called
final
.

API 12
.

Required operations on atomic relations
(
\mathbb{\Lambda}^{\circ}
represents relations over
D^{*}
, i.e., subsets of
D^{*}{\times}D^{*}
).

•

\texttt{atomic}(s\in S)\in\mathbb{\Xi}

\overline{\mathcal{C}(s)}
,

•

\texttt{epsilon}(\Xi\in\mathbb{\Xi})\in\mathbb{\Lambda}^{\circ}

\Xi\cap D^{*}{\times}D^{*}
,

•

\texttt{derivative}(\Xi\in\mathbb{\Xi},s\in S)\in(\mathbb{\Lambda}^{\circ}\times\mathbb{\Xi})^{\leqslant k}

\Xi^{\textup{}(s)}
.

In order to adapt this data structure to represent parsing relations, we need to additionally keep track of transitions.
Instead of the underlying type
\mathbb{\Upsilon}
, let us parameterize it with a type
\mathbb{\Xi}
,
representing a finite family of guided relations, realizing API
12
.
Our implementation is supposed to compute flat derivatives,
let us then introduce an auxiliary
flattening
operation, defined for any
\Lambda\subseteq D^{*}{\times}D^{*}
as

\left[\Lambda\right]\triangleq\{{\hbox to12.59pt{\vbox to12.76pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.29376pt\lower-4.44444pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{
{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.43 9.13 L -7.88 9.13}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{-5.89377pt}{6.59721pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 2.11 C -2.35 0.84 -1.18 0.25 0 0 C -1.18 -0.25 -2.35 -0.84 -2.88 -2.11}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{
{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.07 7.78 L -2.52 7.78}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{-2.02222pt}{5.62498pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 2.11 C -2.35 0.84 -1.18 0.25 0 0 C -1.18 -0.25 -2.35 -0.84 -2.88 -2.11}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}})\in\Lambda\}.

With
\underline{\Psi}
denoting the
\epsilon
-free part of
\Psi
(i.e.,
\Psi-D^{*}{\times}D^{*}
),
we can now show the following generalization of Proposition
11
:

12
.

Let
\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2},\ldots,\Psi_{n}
be a sequence of guided relations over
G^{*}{\times}D^{*}
,
in which each
\Psi_{i}
is obtained by applying one of the operations of API
10
to some
\Psi_{j}
with
j<i
.
Then there exists a finite collection of triples
\{(\Lambda_{j},\Xi_{j},i_{j})\}_{j}

with
\Lambda_{j}\subseteq D^{*}{\times}D^{*}
,
\Xi_{j}\in\mathbb{\Xi}
and
1\leqslant i_{j}<n
, such that

\underline{\Psi_{n}}=\bigcup_{j}\Lambda_{j}{\cdot}\underline{\Xi_{j}}{\cdot}\Psi_{i_{j}}.

Proof.

Again, the only nontrivial operation to be considered in the inductive proof is
derivative
.
This time the inductive step follows from

\displaystyle\underline{\Psi_{n}^{\textup{flat}(s)}}\

\displaystyle=\ \underline{\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\\
(\Lambda,\Xi^{\prime})\in\underline{\Xi_{j}}^{\textup{}(s)}\end{subarray}}\left[\Lambda_{j}{\cdot}\Lambda\right]{\cdot}\Xi^{\prime}{\cdot}\Psi_{i_{j}}}\ =

\displaystyle=\ \bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\\
(\Lambda,\Xi^{\prime})\in\Xi_{j}^{\textup{}(s)}\end{subarray}}\left[\Lambda_{j}{\cdot}\Lambda\right]{\cdot}\underline{\Xi^{\prime}}{\cdot}\Psi_{i_{j}}\ \cup\ \bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\\
(\Lambda,\Xi^{\prime})\in\Xi_{j}^{\textup{}(s)}\end{subarray}}\left[\Lambda_{j}{\cdot}\Lambda\right]{\cdot}(\Xi^{\prime}\cap D^{*}{\times}D^{*}){\cdot}\underline{\Psi_{i_{j}}}.

∎

Applying this result to our DAG structure requires labeling edges with pairs
(\Lambda,\Xi)
, with
\Lambda\subseteq D^{*}{\times}D^{*}
and
\Xi\in\mathbb{\Xi}
.
Instead of the single
\epsilon
bit, vertices need to be labeled with relations over
D^{*}
(subsets of
D^{*}{\times}D^{*}
)—
final
vertices are now those with a nonempty label.

As we can see, for edge and vertex labels we need an additional data type, denoted
\mathbb{\Lambda}
, representing relations over
D^{*}
.
For efficiency reasons, we allow it to be different from the type
\mathbb{\Lambda}^{\circ}
returned from
derivative
and
epsilon
calls on atomic relations.
Realizing our API requires elements of
\mathbb{\Lambda}
to be merged using set union, flattened.
We also require concatenatenation on the left with an atomic
\mathbb{\Lambda}^{\circ}
or with whatever
\Delta\subseteq D^{*}
our
prepend
method is called.
To simplify further reasoning, we combine them into a single operation.
Concatenation on the right with elements of
\mathbb{\Lambda}^{\circ}
is always immediately followed by flattening,
and is therefore redundant, as
\left[\Lambda{\cdot}\Lambda^{\circ}\right]=\left[\left[\Lambda\right]{\cdot}\Lambda^{\circ}{\cdot}\epsilon\right]
.
The requirements are summarized by API
13
.

API 13
.

Required operations on transition relations (subsets of
D^{*}{\times}D^{*}
).

\mathbb{\Delta}
represents languages of transitions (i.e., subsets of
D^{*}
).

•

\texttt{epsilon}()\in\mathbb{\Lambda}

\epsilon
,

•

\texttt{prepend}(\Delta\in\mathbb{\Delta},\Lambda^{\circ}\in\mathbb{\Lambda}^{\circ},\Lambda\in\mathbb{\Lambda})\in\mathbb{\Lambda}

\Delta{\cdot}\Lambda^{\circ}{\cdot}\Lambda
,

•

\texttt{flatten}(\Lambda\in\mathbb{\Lambda})\in\mathbb{\Delta}

\left[\Lambda\right]
,

•

\texttt{union}(\Lambda_{1},\ldots,\Lambda_{k}\in\mathbb{\Lambda})\in\mathbb{\Lambda}

\Lambda_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\Lambda_{k}
.

It is also clear that if the Boolean type
\mathbb{B}
is used in place of
\mathbb{\Lambda}
(and thus also
\mathbb{\Lambda}^{\circ}
and
\mathbb{\Delta}
),
we are left with the simpler DAG useful for recognition.

4.2
Atomic closures

We now turn to the call-reduce closures of individual states, realizing APIs
11
and
12
.
The constructive proof of the proposition below presents a possible direct representation using nondeterministic finite automata.
The non-relational variant could of course be determinized and minimized,
but it might not be advantageous to do so—the price for less nondeterminism is a larger automaton size,
and the grammar-related complexity of our complete algorithm depends on both.

\mathcal{C}(s)=\Phi_{s\rightsquigarrow\epsilon}
.
By definition, the derivatives of any
\Phi_{s\rightsquigarrow\sigma}
can be expressed as

\Phi_{s\rightsquigarrow\sigma}^{\textup{}(u)}=\{(\epsilon,\Phi_{s\rightsquigarrow\sigma{\cdot}u})\}.

This, however, does not directly lead to a
finite
family closed under derivatives.
Instead, we show the following.

13
.

For every
s,t,u\in S
, the derivatives of
\Phi_{s\rightsquigarrow t}
are

\Phi_{s\rightsquigarrow t}^{\textup{}(u)}=\left\{\left(\bigcup_{d=\textup{{call}}\langle t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime},u\rangle\in D}\Delta_{t^{\prime\prime}\rightsquigarrow t}{\cdot}d,\ \Phi_{s\rightsquigarrow t^{\prime}}\right)\right\}_{t^{\prime}\in S}.

, we are interested in

.

which realizes

and split it at the point where
u{\cdot}\sigma^{\prime}
becomes the suffix of the configuration, not to be touched later.

u{\cdot}\sigma^{\prime}
cannot be empty (as then
u
would get touched),
which means that
u
must be the lower state placed on the configration by some call.

\eta

14
.

For each
s\in S
, the language and relation
\mathcal{C}(s)
are
(|S|+1,|S|)
-regular.

Proof.

The definitions of
\mathcal{C}(s)
, derivatives, and regularity for relations
degenerate cleanly to those for languages when specific transitions are forgotten.
Therefore we only need to prove the statement about relations.
Indeed, by the above lemma,
for each
s\in S
, the family containing
\mathcal{C}(s)=\Phi_{s\rightsquigarrow\epsilon}
and
\Phi_{s\rightsquigarrow t}
for every
t\in S

is closed under derivatives of degree
|S|
.
∎

This result can be translated to the null closures of atomic languages and relations:
when taking derivatives, nullable states might need to be skipped over
(with their nulling transitions given in the first component of the
\Lambda
),
which in the language of finite automata corresponds to taking
\epsilon
-closure,
possibly for the price of increased nondeterminism.

15
.

For each
s\in S
, the guided language and relation
\overline{\mathcal{C}(s)}
are
(|S|+1,|S|)
-regular.

Proof.

Again, only the statement about relations needs to be proven.

The required family closed under derivatives are simply the null closures of those given above, i.e.,
\overline{\Phi_{s\rightsquigarrow\epsilon}}
and
\overline{\Phi_{s\rightsquigarrow t}}
.
We would like to show that for some
\Lambda_{u,t,t^{\prime}}\subseteq D^{*}{\times}D^{*}
we have

\overline{\Phi_{s\rightsquigarrow t}}^{\textup{}(u)}=\left\{\left(\Lambda_{u,t,t^{\prime}},\ \overline{\Phi_{s\rightsquigarrow t^{\prime}}}\right)\right\}_{t^{\prime}\in S}.

To express the transition relations
\Lambda_{u,t,t^{\prime}}
which are to be split off when taking derivatives,
let us first give a name to the transition languages from Lemma
13
, denoting

\Delta_{u,t,t^{\prime}}\triangleq\bigcup_{d=\textup{{call}}\langle t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime},u\rangle\in D}\Delta_{t^{\prime\prime}\rightsquigarrow t}{\cdot}d.

Now consider calculating
\overline{\Phi_{s\rightsquigarrow t}}^{\textup{}(u)}
, fix some
t^{\prime}\in S

and imagine a pair
(\gamma,{\hbox to5.37pt{\vbox to10.13pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.68265pt\lower-3.12498pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.48265pt}{-1.18056pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{
{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.44 7.3 L -2.88 7.3}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{-2.28265pt}{5.27776pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 2.11 C -2.35 0.84 -1.18 0.25 0 0 C -1.18 -0.25 -2.35 -0.84 -2.88 -2.11}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}})\in\overline{\Phi_{s\rightsquigarrow t}}
which will participate in this component of the derivative.

The configuration
\gamma
might begin with the state
u
.
In this case, we can simply take the derivative
\Phi_{s\rightsquigarrow t}^{\textup{}(u)}
and null-close the result.
Therefore,
\Lambda_{u,t,t^{\prime}}
must contain
\epsilon{\times}\Delta_{u,t,t^{\prime}}
.

If
\gamma
does not start with
u
, it must begin by a sequence of guiding transitions
\eta^{\prime}
which nulls some state
u^{\prime}
.
The state must have been present on top of the configuration in
\Phi_{s\rightsquigarrow t}
to whose null closure
\gamma
belongs.
Therefore, we might take
\Phi_{s\rightsquigarrow t}^{\textup{}(u^{\prime})}
, moving to
\Phi_{s\rightsquigarrow t^{\prime\prime}}
(note that the state
t^{\prime\prime}
might be different than
t^{\prime}
),
and then recursively calculate the derivative
\overline{\Phi_{s\rightsquigarrow t^{\prime\prime}}}^{\textup{}(u)}
.
Therefore,
\Lambda_{u,t,t^{\prime}}
must also contain
{\hbox to27.97pt{\vbox to13.06pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 13.98576pt\lower-4.59222pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-13.78577pt}{-2.24109pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{
{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}{\times}\Delta_{u^{\prime},t,t^{\prime\prime}}\ {\cdot}\ \Lambda_{u,t^{\prime\prime},t^{\prime}}
.

As there are no other possibilities, we conclude that the relations
\Lambda_{u,t,t^{\prime}}
must be the least (wrt. inclusion) solutions to the equations

\Lambda_{u,t,t^{\prime}}=(\epsilon{\times}\Delta_{u,t,t^{\prime}})\ \cup\ \bigcup_{u^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime}\in S}\left({\hbox to27.97pt{\vbox to13.06pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 13.98576pt\lower-4.59222pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-13.78577pt}{-2.24109pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{
{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.08 9.33 L -18.52 9.33}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{-13.58577pt}{6.745pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 2.11 C -2.35 0.84 -1.18 0.25 0 0 C -1.18 -0.25 -2.35 -0.84 -2.88 -2.11}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}{\times}\Delta_{u^{\prime},t,t^{\prime\prime}}\right){\cdot}\Lambda_{u,t^{\prime\prime},t^{\prime}}.

For the derivative
\overline{\mathcal{C}(s)}^{\textup{}(u)}=\overline{\Phi_{s\rightsquigarrow\epsilon}}^{\textup{}(u)}
, the reasoning is similar:
either we go directly to the target relation
\overline{\Phi_{s\rightsquigarrow u}}
, splitting off nothing (as was the case in Lemma
13
),
or we skip over a nullable state
u^{\prime}
by taking derivative by it (this time this can only move us to
\Phi_{s\rightsquigarrow u^{\prime}}
)
and recursively calculate
\overline{\Phi_{s\rightsquigarrow u^{\prime}}}^{\textup{}(u)}
.
We conclude that

\overline{\mathcal{C}(s)}^{\textup{}(u)}=\{(\epsilon,\overline{\Phi_{s\rightsquigarrow u}})\}\ \cup\ \left\{\left(\bigcup_{u^{\prime}\in S}\left({\hbox to27.97pt{\vbox to13.06pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 13.98576pt\lower-4.59222pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-13.78577pt}{-2.24109pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{
{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.08 9.33 L -18.52 9.33}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{-13.58577pt}{6.745pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 2.11 C -2.35 0.84 -1.18 0.25 0 0 C -1.18 -0.25 -2.35 -0.84 -2.88 -2.11}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}{\times}\epsilon\right){\cdot}\Lambda_{u,u^{\prime},t},\ \overline{\Phi_{s\rightsquigarrow t}}\right)\right\}_{t\in S},

where the singleton case on the left might be incorporated into the family on the right by including the
(\epsilon,\epsilon)
pair in the transition relation for
t=u
.
∎

As a consequence,
once the necessary transition relations and languages have been pre-computed (this might happen at parser generation time),
the closures of grammar states, together with all their derivatives,
might be represented during parsing simply by pairs of states.

We also need to extract the transition languages corresponging to the empty configuration—or, for guided relations, to the configurations containing only guiding transitions and no states.
The first task is simple, because by definition

(\epsilon,{\hbox to5.37pt{\vbox to10.13pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.68265pt\lower-3.12498pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.48265pt}{-1.18056pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

4.3
Transition relations and languages

Moving down the ladder of abstraction,
it is time to find the representations
\mathbb{\Lambda}
and
\mathbb{\Delta}
for transition relations and languages.

For
\mathbb{\Lambda}
, note that the only way its values,
formed from
\epsilon
by chains of calls to
prepend
and
union
,
can ever be examined is by calls to
flatten
.
Consider first a value formed using only
prepend
, i.e., of the form

\Lambda=\Delta_{1}{\cdot}\Lambda^{\circ}_{1}{\cdots}\Delta_{k}{\cdot}\Lambda^{\circ}_{k}.

The flattening of such value can be expressed as

\left[\Lambda\right]=\left[\ldots\left[\Delta_{1}{\cdot}\Lambda^{\circ}_{1}\right]\ldots{\cdot}\Delta_{k}{\cdot}\Lambda^{\circ}_{k}\right].

To perform this computation, it is enough to require the type
\mathbb{\Lambda}^{\circ}
to support a strengthened version of flattening,
which we call
wrapping
:

•

\texttt{wrap}(\Delta\in\mathbb{\Delta},\Lambda^{\circ}\in\mathbb{\Lambda}^{\circ})\in\mathbb{\Delta}

\left[\Delta{\cdot}\Lambda^{\circ}\right]
.

If forming the
\Lambda
in question involved some calls to
union
,
we will make use of the fact that flattening distributes over union.
The whole expression for
\Lambda
can be seen as a DAG with a single distinguished sink node, and edges labeled with calls to
prepend
.
Each path through this DAG represents a union-free expression as discussed above.
The relation represented by a node will be the union over all paths from this node to the sink.
Shall
flatten
be requested for a node
v
representing some
\Lambda_{v}
,
the value can be computed following a topological traversal from
v
,
in time linear in the size of the DAG, and thus in the number of operations used to form
\Lambda_{v}
.

For the atomic relations (i.e., the type
\mathbb{\Lambda}^{\circ}
),
we
“represent”
them directly by the implementation of their
wrap
operation.
We only need to express how the result of
wrap
(for an arbitrary
\Delta
) will look like
for the primitives returned by API
12
, namely

\Lambda_{s\rightsquigarrow\epsilon},\ \Lambda_{s\rightsquigarrow t},\ \Lambda_{u,t,t^{\prime}}\ \text{and}\ \bigcup_{u^{\prime}\in S}\left({\hbox to27.97pt{\vbox to13.06pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 13.98576pt\lower-4.59222pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-13.78577pt}{-2.24109pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{
{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.08 9.33 L -18.52 9.33}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{-13.58577pt}{6.745pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 2.11 C -2.35 0.84 -1.18 0.25 0 0 C -1.18 -0.25 -2.35 -0.84 -2.88 -2.11}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}{\times}\epsilon\right){\cdot}\Lambda_{u,u^{\prime},t}.

This can be given by the following set of recursive equations:

\displaystyle\left[\Delta{\cdot}\Lambda_{u,t,t^{\prime}}\right]

\displaystyle=\Delta{\cdot}\Delta_{u,t,t^{\prime}}\cup\bigcup_{u^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime}\in S}\Delta_{u^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow\epsilon}{\cdot}\left[\Delta{\cdot}\Lambda_{u^{\prime},t,t^{\prime\prime}}\right]{\cdot}\Delta_{u,t^{\prime\prime},t^{\prime}},

\displaystyle\left[\Delta{\cdot}\left(\bigcup_{u^{\prime}\in S}\left({\hbox to27.97pt{\vbox to13.06pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 13.98576pt\lower-4.59222pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-13.78577pt}{-2.24109pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{
{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.08 9.33 L -18.52 9.33}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{-13.58577pt}{6.745pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 2.11 C -2.35 0.84 -1.18 0.25 0 0 C -1.18 -0.25 -2.35 -0.84 -2.88 -2.11}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}{\times}\epsilon\right){\cdot}\Lambda_{u,u^{\prime},t}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{u^{\prime}\in S}\left[\Delta_{u^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow\epsilon}{\cdot}\Delta{\cdot}\Lambda_{u,u^{\prime},t}\right],

\displaystyle\left[\Delta{\cdot}\Lambda_{s\rightsquigarrow t}\right]

\displaystyle=\Delta{\cdot}\Delta_{s\rightsquigarrow t}\cup\bigcup_{u^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime}\in S}\Delta_{u^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow\epsilon}{\cdot}\left[\Delta{\cdot}\Lambda_{u^{\prime},t,t^{\prime\prime}}\right]{\cdot}\Delta_{s\rightsquigarrow t^{\prime\prime}},

\displaystyle\left[\Delta{\cdot}\Lambda_{s\rightsquigarrow\epsilon}\right]

\displaystyle=\Delta{\cdot}\Delta_{s\rightsquigarrow\epsilon}\cup\bigcup_{u^{\prime}\in S}\left[\Delta_{u^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow\epsilon}{\cdot}\Delta{\cdot}\Lambda_{s\rightsquigarrow u^{\prime}}\right].

Of course, it makes sense to permanently remove from the calculation of the above unions those components which are never going to be relevant,
i.e., those for which
\Delta_{u^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow\epsilon}
or
\Delta_{u^{\prime},t,t^{\prime\prime}}
is empty.

API 14
.

Required operations on transition languages (subsets of
D^{*}
)

•

\texttt{epsilon}()\in\mathbb{\Delta}

\{\epsilon\}
,

•

\texttt{concatenate}(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}\in\mathbb{\Delta})\in\mathbb{\Delta}

\Delta_{1}{\cdot}\Delta_{2}
,

•

\texttt{union}(\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{k}\in\mathbb{\Delta})\in\mathbb{\Delta}

\Delta_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\Delta_{k}
,

•

\texttt{is-empty}(\Delta\in\mathbb{\Delta})\in\{\texttt{true},\texttt{false}\}

whether
\Delta=\varnothing
,

•

\texttt{primitive}(?)\in\mathbb{\Delta}

(see text)

As the final piece of data representation, we turn to the type
\mathbb{\Delta}
encoding transition languages.
The basic operations performed on its elements during parsing are union, concatenation, and checking (non)emptyness (cf. API
14
).
However, one also needs a rather rich set of
“primitives”
(which could best be pre-computed during parser generation):
singletons of individual shift transitions,
\Delta_{s\rightsquigarrow\epsilon}
,
\Delta_{s\rightsquigarrow t}
and
\Delta_{u,t,t^{\prime}}
,
and, moreover, the ability to solve equations of the shape given in the above discussion concerning
\mathbb{\Lambda}
.
We employ the equation solver (to be discussed shortly) to compute the primitives by expressing

\displaystyle\Delta_{s\rightsquigarrow\epsilon}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{d=\textup{{reduce}}\langle s,p\rangle\in D}d\ \cup\ \bigcup_{d=\textup{{call}}\langle s,u,t\rangle\in D}\Delta_{t\rightsquigarrow\epsilon}{\cdot}\Delta_{u\rightsquigarrow\epsilon}{\cdot}d,

\displaystyle\Delta_{s\rightsquigarrow t}

\displaystyle=\{\epsilon:\ s=t\}\ \cup\ \bigcup_{d=\textup{{call}}\langle s,u,s^{\prime}\rangle\in D}\Delta_{s^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow t}{\cdot}\Delta_{u\rightsquigarrow\epsilon}{\cdot}d,\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\Delta_{u,t,t^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{d=\textup{{call}}\langle t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime},u\rangle\in D}\Delta_{t^{\prime\prime}\rightsquigarrow t}{\cdot}d.

This way, we are only left with individual transitions, concatenation, union, and equations expressible in terms of these.

To assess the required capabilities of the equation solver, we will analyze the dependency graph between variables.
In particular, when this graph turns out to be acyclic, the solutions might be calculated simply by evaluation.
This time, we start with the variables at the end of the dependency chain.

\Delta_{s\rightsquigarrow\epsilon}
.

Every
\textup{{call}}\langle s,u,t\rangle\in D
seems to induce a dependency between
\Delta_{s\rightsquigarrow\epsilon}
and each of
\Delta_{u\rightsquigarrow\epsilon}
and
\Delta_{t\rightsquigarrow\epsilon}
.
However, this dependency is only real if
\Delta_{t\rightsquigarrow\epsilon}
(respectively,
\Delta_{u\rightsquigarrow\epsilon}
) is nonempty,
which is exactly when
t
(respectively,
u
) is nullable.
Let us then assume that nullability has been computed by some other means (see, e.g.,
[
10
]
, Section 4.2),
and announce a
right dependency

\Delta_{s\rightsquigarrow\epsilon}\succ\Delta_{t\rightsquigarrow\epsilon}
if
\textup{{call}}\langle s,u,t\rangle\in D
for some nullable
u
,
and a
left-right dependency

\Delta_{s\rightsquigarrow\epsilon}\succ\Delta_{u\rightsquigarrow\epsilon}
if
\textup{{call}}\langle s,u,t\rangle\in D
for some nullable
t
.
The direction(s) of dependencies record on which side of their targets a nonempty sequence of transitions might occur.

\Delta_{s\rightsquigarrow t}
.

Dependencies on
\Delta_{s\rightsquigarrow\epsilon}
are irrelevant, as they will never introduce cycles.
The only internal dependencies in this layer are
a right dependency
\Delta_{s\rightsquigarrow t}\succ\Delta_{s^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow t}
if
\textup{{call}}\langle s,u,s^{\prime}\rangle\in D
for some nullable
u
.
Note that it occurs in accord with an identical dependency
\Delta_{s\rightsquigarrow\epsilon}\succ\Delta_{s^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow\epsilon}
.

\left[\Delta{\cdot}\Lambda_{u,t,t^{\prime}}\right]
.

The dependency graph between these variables is independent of
\Delta

(unless
\Delta=\varnothing
, in which case the solution is trivially
\varnothing
)
and also of
t
,
let us then omit them from node descriptions.
We have a left-right dependency
\Lambda_{u,t^{\prime}}\succ\Lambda_{u^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime}}
if and only if

u^{\prime}
is nullable and
\Delta_{u,t^{\prime\prime},t^{\prime}}\neq\varnothing
, which in turns happens if we have a
\textup{{call}}\langle t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime\prime},u\rangle\in D
with
\Delta_{t^{\prime\prime\prime}\rightsquigarrow t^{\prime\prime}}\neq\varnothing
.
Note, that a left-right dependency
\Delta_{s\rightsquigarrow\epsilon}\succ\Delta_{u\rightsquigarrow\epsilon}
, witnessed by a nullable
t
as discussed above, implies
\Lambda_{t,s}\succ\Lambda_{t,u}
.

Other variables introduce no potentially cyclic dependencies.
This means there are only two ways in which a cycle can appear, making solution-by-evaluation impossible:

1.

There is a cycle of left-right dependencies between the nodes denoted
\Lambda_{u,t}
.
Consider such a cycle
\Lambda_{u_{1},t_{1}}\succ\Lambda_{u_{2},t_{2}}\succ\ldots\succ\Lambda_{u_{r},t_{r}}=\Lambda_{u_{0},t_{1}}
,
and imagine a successful run of the RTN in which the state
t_{1}
appears.
The history of any such run must look as follows:

\lxSVG@closescope {{

Iterating this trick, one obtains an infinite set of accepting transition sequences for the same input.
Moreover, as the insertions on the left and right of
\delta_{2}
need to be balanced (this is the reason we call such dependencies left-right),
the language of those accepting runs is not regular (it is obviously context-free).

Together, either every state
t_{i}
on such cycle is useless and could be removed, or the RTN is
nonregularly infinitely ambiguous
.
One could argue that such grammars could be immediately rejected during parser generation
5
5

5

That is what our proof-of-concept implementation does.

(the check for this situation is fairly simple).
If one decides to handle them, the type
\mathbb{\Delta}
needs to be equipped with some mechanism for handling context-free (nonregular) structures.

2.

There is a cycle of joint right and left-right dependencies between the nodes
\Delta_{s\rightsquigarrow\epsilon}
.
Again, one could argue that either the whole such cycle is useless, or the RTN is infinitely ambiguous.
However, if all such cycles consist of right dependencies only, the situation is somewhat less complicated:
mutual dependencies exist
only
between the variables
\Delta_{s\rightsquigarrow\epsilon}
, and all those dependencies are left-linear.
In such case, the languages in question are regular (albeit still infinite) and the only additional feature required of the type
\mathbb{\Delta}
is the Kleene star operator.

From the discussion above we can see that, unless one needs to handle infinitely ambiguous grammars,
the requirements on
\mathbb{\Delta}
are quite small:
the
primitive
operaton in API
14
only needs to handle individual transitions.

This leaves a wide spectrum of implementation possibilities.
As mentioned previously, one can instantiate
\mathbb{\Delta}
to
\mathbb{B}
, obtaining a recognition algorithm.
On the other extreme, one can use a free algebra over
concatenate
and
union
—the result will be an encoding of all parse trees, very similar in spirit to the SPPF structure employed by polynomial-time generalized parsers.
Both of the above require
O(1)
time per operation on
\mathbb{\Delta}
.

There are, however, other options.
In particular, one may keep singleton languages only, replacing larger ones by a unique ambiguity marker.
The result is a de-generalized parser, finding the unique parse tree for its input (or reporting ambiguity).
If the languages are remembered up to (and including) the first ambiguity, the location and nature of the ambiguity can be reported to the user.

Further, one can introduce a partial ordering of transitions,
and
“prune”
each intermediate language to contain only lexicographically-maximal transition sequences.
This achieves transition prioritization,
useful among others for expressing operator associativity and precedence,
and eager/lazy versions of repetition constructs.
All of this happens without any modification to the parsing algorithm itself!

5
Arbitrary semiring parsing

If we restrict our attention to finitely ambiguous RTNs,
the API we require of transition languages
\mathbb{\Delta}
is simply the one of a semiring.
In fact, the alternatives we sketched in Section
4.3
are just special semirings.
Substituting
\mathbb{\Delta}
with an arbitrary semiring encounters, however, a hidden limitation—the algorithm, as presented above, requires semiring addition to be idempotent.
Lifting this limitation would allow faster calculation of other useful features (cf.
[
6
]
), e.g., the number of distinct parse trees.
We will now show how it can be achieved.

Let us then assume that
(\mathbb{\Delta},{\cup},\varnothing,{\cdot},\epsilon)
is an arbitrary semiring,
and that a
valuation function

\langle\relbar\rangle:D\to\mathbb{\Delta}
assigns values to individual transitions.
Valuation can be naturally extended to a semiring homomorphism
between the set of languages over
D
(i.e., subsets of
D^{*}
) and the semiring
\mathbb{\Delta}
:

\langle\Delta\rangle\triangleq\bigcup_{d_{1}\ldots d_{k}\in\Delta}\langle d_{1}\rangle{\cdots}\langle d_{k}\rangle.

We would like our parsing algorithm to return, for an input
\alpha
, the value

\langle\{\delta{\cdot}\eta:(\eta,{\hbox to4.84pt{\vbox to10.82pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-3.47221pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.22221pt}{-3.47221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{
{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.07 7.78 L -2.52 7.78}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{-2.02222pt}{5.62498pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 2.11 C -2.35 0.84 -1.18 0.25 0 0 C -1.18 -0.25 -2.35 -0.84 -2.88 -2.11}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}})\in\overline{\Phi_{\alpha}}\}\rangle.

Valuation can be pre-computed for the
“building blocks”
of the languages of transitions
(individual transitions, nullings of individual states, and whatever is needed for the derivatives of
\overline{\mathcal{C}(s)}
),
and then, being a homomorphism, pushed through the algorithm to its final result.
It might happen, however, that at some point of the computation,
the same sequence of transitions appears as a member of both arguments of a union operation—we will say that such computation is
redundant
.
If
\cup
is idempotent (as set union is), this does not hurt. If it is not, such a sequence will be
“counted twice”
.
Therefore, to make the algorithm work correctly over an arbitrary semiring, we must assure that such a situation never takes place.

Redundancy may be potentially introduced wherever a union of transition languages appears in the algorithm, namely when:

•

(pre)computing the valuation of transition relations and languages needed by the derivatives of
\overline{\mathcal{C}(s)}
,

•

computing derivatives of degree
k
—if the same pair appears in more than one of the
k
components, or

•

composing
\Psi_{\textup{out}}
or
\Psi_{\texttt{aux}}
in Algorithm
8
.

The first case we have already avoided—in the equations considered in Section
4.3
,
the sets of transition sequences in each member of each union are guaranteed to be disjoint.
The second is an additional requirement to be put on the API for guided relations—a requirement which the representation proposed in Section
4.1
also already satisfies.
The remaining part of this section addresses the third case.

As it stands, Algorithm
7
(and thus the equivalent Algorithm
8
) is redundant in two ways.
Firstly, some sequences of transitions (and their corresponding configurations) may be added to
\Psi_{\texttt{out}}
both in lines 4 and 7.
Secondly, when it is used iteratively by Algorithm
9
,
some sequences of transitions have multiple ways in which they can be split between successive iterations.

It turns out that a careful adjustment of the nulled atomic closure relations
\overline{\mathcal{C}(s)}
is enough to completely get rid of redundancy—neither the top-level parsing algorithm, nor the API (or implementation) of guided relations
\mathbb{\Psi}
needs to be modified.
Formally, we change the meaning of call-reduce closure never to include the empty configuration:

\mathcal{C}(\Psi)\triangleq\{(\gamma^{\prime},\eta{\cdot}\delta):\ \gamma\mathrel{\hbox to28.73pt{\vbox to18.51pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 14.36333pt\lower-12.31284pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
